Guatemala’s Democracy Dilemma
2026-09-20

Guatemala’s Democracy Dilemma

The Atlantic | 2026-09-20

Visit Original Website
Just over three years ago in Guatemala, a coalition of young and Indigenous voters helped propel Bernardo Arévalo, a reformer who campaigned against corruption, to an astounding presidential win. Guatemala had not only refused the global trend toward populist authoritarianism; it had also defied what its citizens called “the pact of the corrupt,” a loose alliance of politicians, judges, criminal networks, and crony capitalists that had gained control of the country’s institutions. Today the mood in Guatemala is less hopeful. Some of Arévalo’s former allies, and many of those who voted for him, are dejected, convinced that the president missed a once-in-a-generation opportunity to transform the country. When I met with Arévalo in Guatemala City in May, he defended the pace of his administration’s progress and suggested that the alternative would have been to abuse the powers of a government he was elected to reform. In Latin America, “we never truly got used to understanding that the rule of law is fundamental,” he told me. “Rule of law is not the same as rule by law. Rule of law is when we all accept that we are subject to the law and we operate within that framework to solve problems. Rule by law is when I use the law that’s at my disposal as part of my political arsenal.” Had he crossed that line, Arévalo insisted, he would have risked being ousted by his opponents and replaced by someone willing to do much worse. His critics disagree. Arévalo’s personal integrity is undisputed. But some of the reformers who helped elect him now argue that he may be too scrupulously rules-respecting to deal effectively with the “pact of the corrupt” and the institutions it captured—too much of a liberal democrat to save democracy. When he leaves office in 2028, they fear that the pact will quickly retake control of the few institutions it has lost, or that voters will choose an autocrat who promises to crush crime without concern for legality. Guatemala’s predicament points to a more universal problem. Can liberal reformers unwind authoritarian state capture without, themselves, engaging in at least a little bit of illiberalism? The dilemma is not Guatemala’s alone. Move too aggressively, and you risk being branded an autocrat, or even becoming one. Move too cautiously, and you risk obstruction, sabotage, and ineffectuality. South Africa’s Cyril Ramaphosa chose a gradualist approach to reversing Jacob Zuma’s state capture and disappointed many of his supporters. Placing the opposite bet, Poland’s Donald Tusk bent rules to try to undo the damage done by the right-populist Law and Justice party—and stood accused of undermining the rule of law. Hungary’s post-Orbán leader, Péter Magyar, seems to be following Tusk’s example. In a way, both Arévalo and his critics are right. Guatemala has avoided a democratic breakdown. But the president’s rules-bound approach has also left many Guatemalans feeling that he has not fought for them forcefully enough. The question now is whether Arévalo has preserved democracy as an ideal that Guatemalans believe in and, if necessary, will defend. In Guatemala City, I watched as hundreds of people gathered outside the country’s public prosecutor’s office, a bunker-like building ringed by a huge black-steel fence put up three years before, allegedly to shield the occupants from nonviolent democracy protesters. The crowd had come to celebrate the long-awaited end to the term of María Consuelo Porras, the country’s attorney general and, to many citizens, the personification of the pact of the corrupt. For the past eight years, Porras had been a figure of fear. She used the power of her position to investigate and jail critics of state corruption. When Arévalo was elected in 2023, her office tried to overturn the results, alleging irregularities that international observers found baseless. That effort looked likely to succeed—until indigenous Maya communities organized peaceful demonstrations that lasted 106 days and drew the participation of nearly 2 million Guatemalans. Arévalo was inaugurated. That he did not take extralegal action to remove Porras from her post has given rise to some of the fiercest criticism against him. She opened an investigation into Arévalo’s party, the Seed Movement, for relatively minor party-registration irregularities. Although the probe did not produce formal charges, it led to the party’s dissolution and thus thwarted any hope of its controlling Congress. Porras also brought more than 75 legal cases or complaints against the president and his supporters, including the arrests of two of the Indigenous leaders behind the 2023 protests, and even a cabinet minister. Now Porras was finally leaving, and a new attorney general was taking over. The Guatemalans gathered outside the office had waited for this moment. What they had not anticipated was how long Porras’s departure would take, and how few people would come out to celebrate it. Thousands had massed in the same street just a few years before. When I met Arévalo a few days later, he justified the delay by explaining that his government had arrived “with an enormous level of public legitimacy, but without control of the Congress, and with the justice system positioned against us.” Legally, he had no authority to simply eject the attorney general. He sent a bill to Congress to grant him that power, but the opposition blocked it. “If, in that scenario, I make a political miscalculation, the result isn’t that I go with a hundred people to pull Consuelo Porras out of that building and that this is sustainable and produces results,” he said. It was that “I go to prison or into exile, and someone else comes in, one of them, to take power.” Like much else in Guatemala, the colossal, mint-green presidential palace where Arévalo sat down with me is a holdover from a bygone era of authoritarianism. It was built by Guatemala’s Hitler-admiring, yet U.S.-backed dictator, Jorge Ubico, who rose to power in 1931 and was forced to resign amid mass protests in 1944. Arévalo, before winning the presidency, was best known as the son of the man who succeeded Ubico, in Guatemala’s first truly free elections. Juan José Arévalo, an educator and a social democrat, returned to Guatemala from exile in 1944 to run for president against the ex-dictator’s favored candidate. As his son would later, he won by a landslide, and Guatemala’s “democratic spring” began, a time of expanded rights and education. But after his freely and fairly elected successor, Jacobo Arbenz, went further, carrying out a land reform that threatened the interests of wealthy families and the powerful U.S.-based United Fruit Company, the United States organized a coup that put the military in power in 1954. Nine years later, a guerrilla leader asked Arévalo to help spearhead armed resistance to the regime, and the former president refused, saying that confronting the military head-on would turn Guatemala “into a lake of blood.” He was right. In the name of crushing the greatly outnumbered guerrillas, the military and its allies killed or disappeared more than 100,000 Guatemalans over the next three decades. It massacred leftists, labor leaders, and residents of more than 400 Maya villages suspected of supporting insurgents. Guatemalan courts eventually deemed the latter crime a genocide. Bernardo Arévalo, born in exile in Uruguay, returned with his family in the middle of the dictatorship period, and he began a career as a diplomat at its tail end, as the country formally returned to elections and civilian rule in 1985. He later left the diplomatic service, earned a Ph.D., and worked in international peace-building. The wood-paneled presidential office where we met was decorated only with two portraits: his father’s, in black-and-white, and his own, beside it, in color. Arévalo’s demeanor was professorial. “We are in a process of recovery,” he said. “Democracy doesn’t develop in a linear, unidirectional way; it has leaps and twists, not only here, but everywhere in the world.” For much of the 1990s and 2000s, Guatemala had been twisting its way toward democracy, but corruption networks and drug traffickers had insinuated themselves into the country’s institutions. “Part of what we set out to do is to recognize the serious damage this had caused the country—this corrosion, this penetration of political-criminal networks” that served no “ideological interest,” Arévalo said, but rather “the highest bidder: whoever could pay for impunity.” Dislodging these forces from the machinery of the state has been neither swift nor straightforward, and even those most anxious for change are hard-pressed to offer practical solutions. The president told me that some supporters had asked him to use the military to remove Porras. He found the suggestion unsettling: “Are you really asking us to go back to the years of military dictatorships of the 1970s, when armies were the only solution?” Read: A new history of the Western Hemisphere I asked Arévalo about the Indigenous protest leaders, Luis Pacheco and Hector Chaclán, who had then been imprisoned for more than a year while under investigation on terrorism charges. “I would like the people who say ‘But the government could have done something else’ to tell me what,” Arévalo replied, exasperated. “What they’re venting is the frustration we all feel.” He assured me that the pact of the corrupt was weakening incrementally as its networks lost access to the state: first the executive branch, then the public-works contracting system, now the prosecutor’s office. “Without tools, they aren’t powerful. And our task is to keep removing those tools from them.” Arévalo can point to some successes. Young lawyers have worked to clean up corruption in the judicial selection process. Appointed in March, the new bench of the  Constitutional Court, which rules on the constitutionality of laws and policies, is more favorable to reformers than the one it replaced, as is the new electoral tribunal, which oversees elections. I spoke with the new attorney general a few days after he assumed his post: He was so busy firing Porras’s apparatchiks and preparing for the institution to resume serious criminal investigations that he was sleeping only four or five hours a night. An anti-corruption commission that Arévalo formed early on has already filed more than 200 criminal complaints against former officials. Arévalo was tasked with not only freeing a state captured by kleptocrats, but also strengthening one that was fundamentally weak. The bureaucracy he inherited had little capacity to provide public services, reduce poverty, or fight organized crime. In this, too, his administration has made some progress. It has hired more than 15,000 new teachers, and built or refurbished more than 150 rural-health clinics (his predecessor built just six in four years, Arévalo said). The government has also replaced dirt floors with cement in more than 75,000 households in the country’s poorest rural municipalities, and has distributed water filters and cleaner-burning stoves. Still, much else that Guatemalans had hoped for has failed to materialize. Congress, which remains dominated by the old-guard pact forces, has blocked or stalled bills aimed at curbing corruption—for example, bills to modernize public-works contracting, require shell companies to disclose who owns them, and protect whistleblowers. Partly because the contracting system remains dysfunctional, the government has made little visible progress toward repairing the country’s crumbling highways. And, unwilling to buy votes in Congress outright, as his predecessors did, Arévalo has resorted to pork-barrel spending and raising congressional salaries to win legislative support. Such tactics are deeply unpopular with the public. Another limiting factor has been the Trump administration. Although Arévalo’s cooperation on crime and drugs has kept the relationship cordial, his opponents have courted President Trump to try to tip the scales against reformers. In April, the U.S. ambassador to Guatemala personally called reluctant Guatemalan lawmakers to pressure them to approve a Constitutional Court judge favored by pact elites and conservatives, a top lawmaker, Luis Aguirre, told me. This tied the court between reformers and their opponents, leaving Arévalo without a reliable majority. The young reformers who campaigned with Arévalo knew that he would face constraints, but some wish that he had more often tested those limits. Samuel Pérez, 34, is a member of Congress; he was also the head of the Seed Movement bloc in Congress before it was dissolved. I went to see him in a building plastered with missing-persons flyers for those who were disappeared during the military dictatorship decades ago. The flyers are a common sight in Guatemala City, posted and reposted by the victims’ families. Pérez told me that Arévalo was elected not only “to overthrow the regime, but also to construct something new, and that isn’t happening.” Now Pérez was creating a party, called Raíces, to which most of the lawmakers formerly associated with the Seed Movement had defected. They would still support Arévalo, but they would advocate for a more confrontational approach. “The president interprets power and good government as doing things a little better and a little more than what already existed. For me, no. You don’t use power to administrate what’s already there, but to transform what isn’t working,” Pérez explained. I’d last seen Pérez during Arévalo’s presidential campaign in 2023. He’d told me then that if the Seed Movement won, the government’s greatest difficulty would be managing popular expectations. But he hadn’t predicted that he himself would be among the disappointed. Arévalo should have rallied the public to pressure Congress to pass the law removing Porras, Pérez said. He should have conceded less to business elites, and done more to free Pacheco and Chaclán. Pérez was particularly upset that the police had come to arrest the two men in a white van with tinted windows—a symbol of the dictatorship, which used such vehicles to disappear its victims—and that the government had not stopped this. Several young Guatemalans in the reform movement suggested to me that the political caution of their parents’ generation was a product of the fear instilled in them during the years of military rule and internal armed conflict. Some speculated that Arévalo’s aversion to escalation emanates from that experience, too, given his youth in exile and the persecution of many of his father’s supporters in the years after the 1954 coup. “I think he is a son of his circumstances,” said Andrea Reyes, a young lawmaker helping Raíces organize. Arévalo readily locates his most formative influences in that era—as a source not of fear, but of strengthened conviction. He spoke to me of his father’s refusal to abandon democratic principles: “Even when the world began to change around him, he kept his convictions, which today we see were the correct convictions, after all, 80 years later.” Arévalo, too, has found his convictions under pressure—not only from former allies who worry that his reforms have gone too slowly, but also from constituents and conservative politicians who want a government that will ignore constitutional limits in cracking down on crime. In this they compare him unfavorably with the autocrat next door: Many Guatemalans who live in daily fear of extortion and kidnapping see gangs, not illiberal state capture, as the biggest threat to their basic freedoms, and they see President Nayib Bukele’s El Salvador as a beacon of safety. Read: El Salvador’s exceptional prison state Bukele, first elected in 2019, took an almost opposite approach from Arévalo’s to governing. He purged institutions of his political rivals, packed them with loyalists, and then cut secret deals with gang leaders before cracking down on their followers. His mass roundups virtually eliminated violent crime in his country, by jailing more than 90,000 people suspected of gang ties, many without due process. Arévalo is unwilling to pursue such methods. He has, however, seized record quantities of cocaine, extradited crime bosses, hired thousands of new police officers, and begun building the country’s first new maximum-security prison in 40 years. But murder and extortion rates have proved stubborn, and many are now demanding more-radical methods. “Guatemalans are searching for their Bukele,” said Alvaro Arzú Escobar, a conservative lawmaker sanctioned by the Biden administration for allegedly trying to undermine Arévalo’s inauguration. Arévalo “isn’t radical in his ideas or his actions,” Arzú told me. But, he added, “the country’s biggest problem right now is a lack of authority. As president, he doesn’t exercise the power he holds. There is a lot of rhetoric, but little action.” The Mariscal Zavala military prison sits on a pine-covered ridge on the outskirts of Guatemala City. A former army base, it houses the state’s highest-profile prisoners. I went there to meet Pacheco and Chaclán, the Indigenous leaders who’d played such an instrumental role in safeguarding the 2023 presidential election. Both had been on the board of the 48 Cantones of Totonicapán—one of the country’s oldest Indigenous self-governing bodies, in rural western Guatemala—when the attorney general’s office had moved to annul the vote. Pacheco and Chaclán canvassed rural villages on foot, helping gather some 50,000 signatures calling for Porras’s resignation. Later they helped organize protests in the capital. “The 48 Cantones is also a democracy, with elected leaders and term limits,” Chaclán told me. “For us, democracy is sacred, and although Guatemala often doesn’t reflect it much, we must respect it.” In April 2025, Porras ordered him and Pacheco into pretrial detention. After that, their court hearings were repeatedly delayed, seemingly intentionally, and when I met them in May, they had no idea when the next one would be. I asked them whether they believed Arévalo should have rallied protesters or found some other tool to remove Porras, and thus to free them. Pacheco responded, “I would have liked it at first, but later I realized it would have only changed the person, not the form of doing justice.” Chaclán added, “This is nothing simple, and surely he is doing what is best for the country. We have faith we have already passed the majority of our time here.” Chaclán was right. On August 5, a judge released Pacheco and Chaclán to house arrest, reuniting them with their families. Around the same time, the prison-like, black-steel fence was taken down from around the public prosecutor’s office. Guatemala’s next presidential election is just nine months away. Right-wing candidates are beginning to emerge, some imitating Bukele, and many sure to court the White House, in the hope that Trump will try to tip the scales. Arévalo’s and Pérez’s reformists claim that they will join forces behind a unity candidate, but such a figure has not yet surfaced. Democracy is twisting its way forward, in Arévalo’s words. No one thinks it is leaping. But the passage of time may provide a different perspective. On one of my last days in Guatemala City, I walked by a cathedral I had passed dozens of times before. I had never previously noticed the tiny engraved names of more than 13,000 victims of the internal armed conflict, so small as to be illegible from even a short distance, on its stone columns. For much of the 20th century, Guatemalans who advocated for self-government, equality before the law, and the welfare of the majority—that is, social democrats—knew that the state might try to kill them. Today, social democrats govern. Reformers may disagree as to whether Bernardo Arévalo made the most of the opportunity he was given. But that such an opportunity could ever arise is more than many members of his father’s generation likely thought possible. *Image Sources: Luis Soto / AP; Johan Ordonez / AFP / Getty; Arnulfo Franco / AFP / Getty.
The Atlantic
190 Days Without a Homicide
2026-09-20

190 Days Without a Homicide

The Atlantic | 2026-09-20

Visit Original Website
Jenee Gragston dropped to the ground and held on to the grass as if it could save her life. She yelled to her family members to get down. She later told me that she didn’t know where the bullets were coming from, but she knew they were close. When the firing stopped, the cries began. She could hear her 4-year-old nephew howling: “Get it off me! Get it off me!” She wasn’t close enough to see what had happened, but it looked like he had blood on his chest. A teenage boy was lying near the child. The teenager “never screamed or anything,” she said. “I can still see all of it.” It was a Saturday afternoon, mid-August in Lexington, Kentucky. Hundreds of people had gathered in Charles Young Park to celebrate what’s called East End Day—a combination of family reunions, live music, and lots of food. I grew up going to festivals in that park, though I lived on the west side of town, in the Charlotte Court housing projects. Kentucky isn’t the most dangerous place in America, but it’s up there, with the 14th-highest rate of gun deaths. Twelve years ago, my uncle was murdered in Lexington. Three years ago, my sister’s husband was shot and killed there. I worry about my brother, who still lives there. He had gotten himself off the streets for a while, but is now back living in the city. Lexington had recently seen a hard-won drop in gun deaths—last year was the fourth straight year of declines in gun violence among young people. This spring and summer set a record of 190 straight days without a homicide. Then came the East End Day shooting, which killed that teenager, 16-year-old Di’Zhon Robertson, and wounded four others—including the 4-year-old, who was shot in the leg. The next weekend, another person was killed. The weekend after that, four separate shootings left two more dead. Gragston was selling Polish sausages in the park. She’d paid more than $300 for the grill, $300 for the sausages, more than $1,000 in fees, $75 for the booth. Business was good; some people returned for seconds. Her husband, her kids, and other family members had come to help her set up her tent. Her 4-year-old nephew was handing customers sodas and water bottles. On the basketball court nearby, Devine Carama was talking with a group of young people. Carama is the director of ONE Lexington, a city-funded program that tries to prevent gun violence by mentoring at-risk boys. He, too, threw himself down when he heard gunfire. “I almost got shot that day,” he told me. Two people near him were hit—a man and his girlfriend. Carama and others tried to bandage the man’s leg; he told me he could see a glimmer of bullet or bone through the blood. Sari Horwitz: The gun lobby’s most cynical move yet A day later, 18-year-old Jametrius Griffin turned himself in and was charged with murder. According to court testimony, Griffin’s younger brother had gotten into an argument; punches were thrown, and the younger brother fell or was pushed. When Griffin saw that, he yanked out a gun and started firing. For the past three years, Robertson had participated in ONE Lexington’s summer youth program, which offers workshops on building emotional and social skills, as well as college-campus visits. “He wasn’t a street kid,” Carama said. Griffin’s younger brother was in ONE Lexington, too—in its school mentoring program. The organization had also helped Griffin get a job at UPS. The group’s work seemed to be making a difference. And then a fight left one young man dead, one wounded, and one in prison. In many ways, Lexington feels like a small town, where everyone knows everyone. I went to middle school with Griffin’s lawyer, Daniel Whitley, who has a big reputation locally for representing people accused of murder—and for winning their cases. The week after the shooting, I spoke with Whitley and with Griffin’s mother, Jay-Me Baxter, on a video call. “A bunch of bullshit” was how Baxter described the park shooting, “because everybody’s friends and everybody’s family.” She said her youngest son told her he had been bullied by Di’Zhon Robertson and others. The day after the shooting, she made the same argument in a video she posted on Facebook that outraged many in the community. She suggested that her older son had had no choice and said she didn’t care “who makes it home as long as mine do.” (I tried to reach Robertson’s mother through family members to see if she would talk with me about what happened to her son; her sister replied that the family didn’t want to relive it. But friends and family members described the teenager as quiet, observant, and “very respectful.”) “I’m like, Shit, I know Di’Zhon’s mama,’” Baxter told me—the other mother was close friends with her own sister. Baxter said she had had a conversation with her about the beef between the boys, and assumed the issue had been settled. Griffin loved football, and he had started college at Kentucky State University. “But you know them kids,” she said. “They be doing stuff in silence.” Her son must have been simmering with fury. I knew Baxter’s family growing up—she’s a year older than me, and they, too, lived in the Charlotte Court housing projects. She didn’t remember me, but she knew my brother. Her mother was white, and had nine children. Baxter, the third-oldest, is half Black, like her younger siblings. But the oldest two are white. Her mother used to cry, Baxter told me, because “the white kids didn’t ever live with us.” In our majority-Black neighborhood, a white mother with mixed-race kids stood out. Baxter remembers people throwing rocks through their windows and a lot of name-calling—“white bitch.” Her family was poorer than many others, but people in the neighborhood assumed that she was privileged, and tormented her for it. “That white mama, man,” she said. “You know what I’m saying?” That kind of bullying is part of the pain of being impoverished. You feel so low, you desperately want to make someone else feel lower. Baxter is a single mother, and Griffin is the oldest of her five children. Like my own mother, she lost custody of her kids because of drugs—she signed over her parental rights to her mom. She told me the story as if she was discussing a character in a book: “Jay-Me got in trouble. Jay-Me started using drugs, cocaine specifically.” Like my mother, Baxter was a victim of sexual abuse. She grew up watching her mother “get beat by men. I done been touched by men, and I never told my mama until a couple months before she died.” It took Baxter seven years to stop using drugs and get her kids back. Those kids needed her, yet she kept choosing drugs over them, she said, as her voice cracked and tears hit her cheeks. “That hurts, because I can’t believe I did that.” I’ve heard those same words from my own mother and watched her cry for the same reason. From the October 2023 issue: Jenisha from Kentucky Before Baxter signed over her rights, she said, she asked her mother to promise that what she’d experienced as a little girl wouldn’t happen to her kids. She wanted to break the cycle of violence, poverty, and dysfunction that is the inheritance of so many children. “I’m not a gun mama,” Baxter told me, but she believes in “stepping in the street” to fight. “I’ve been fighting all my life,” she said. Backgrounds like these are so common in Whitley’s line of work that he seemed to tune out our conversation. At one point on the video call, we heard the sound of snoring. Baxter joked about it: “Listen to him. My lawyer tiiiiiired.” We continued talking for a few more minutes and never heard from him again. “Night night, Daniel,” she said before we hung up. He called me back later, apologized for not saying goodbye, and said he hadn’t been asleep. Despite the pity I felt for Baxter and her family, I felt more for those shot by her son, and their families. Di’Zhon Robertson’s uncle Tyrone Johnson officiated his funeral, and told me that the church was full of people “struggling with their emotions.” I was told by attendees that the teenager’s grieving mother had to be pulled off the coffin. Damon Williams Jr., a 14-year-old wounded in the shooting, was hit in the thigh. He had been planning on joining his middle school’s football team this season. Lakeisha Martin was shot in the stomach and the sternum. East End Day, her older brother Isaiah Barron told me, “is a day for our community that we look forward to every year.” Martin was in the hospital for 21 days and will be unable to work for months. The day of the shooting, Jenee Gragston rushed to the hospital as her nephew went into surgery. She told me the police said she should leave her tent, and could return later to retrieve everything. But when her husband went back to the park, almost everything was gone. The cooler with the meat. The grill, the propane tank, even her prescription eyeglasses. Other tents had been robbed as well. Gragston told me that she was grateful her nephew was okay, but that she would never go to one of the park festivals again. Lexington is trying hard to reduce this kind of gun violence. Mayor Linda Gorton told me the city has handed out free gun locks and offered to buy back guns from residents, among other interventions. Through ONE Lexington, it offers groceries, hotel stays, and educational funds to people affected by gun violence. The city relocated a family whose house had been shot up, Gorton said. Still, she conceded, “there are times when we cannot control every human being’s behavior.” It doesn’t help that Kentucky has such lax gun laws. Just this year, state legislators dropped the age required to carry a concealed firearm from 21 to 18. When I was growing up in Lexington, gun violence was even worse. Whenever you have a community of young men who feel powerless and disrespected, who have been raised to protect themselves and their families from perceived threats, and who believe words can’t save them but a gun might, then death will follow. The story of what happened in Lexington this summer is particularly tragic because so many had worked so long and so carefully to make a difference. But a gun changes everything in a moment. Carama, of ONE Lexington, told me he has more friends who are dead than alive. He, too, said that “a lot of it is out of our control,” and that there’s only so much someone from outside a young person’s family can do. You can monitor a kid all you like, but if he has a gun, “you got a split second to react.” Ultimately, he said, “you can’t prevent everything. It’s just not possible.” In Jay-Me Baxter’s account, she tried, in her way, to intervene. “The mamas grew up together,” she told me. “This could’ve been prevented.” But it wasn’t.

The Brain Is Actually Two Completely Separate Organs
2026-09-20

The Brain Is Actually Two Completely Separate Organs

Slashdot | 2026-09-20

Visit Original Website
It's been known for a while that the nervous system of animals started out as separate front/sensory and rear/motor+autonomic-functions, and in humans we see the same gene expression markers in front/back (fore/midbrain vs hindbrain aka brainstem) as we see in primitive animals such as the acorn worm where these remain far more separate. What this new research shows isn't that our brain originated as two organs/systems, but rather that it still goes through that stage during embryonic development rather than I thought this was common knowledge? The so-called "reptilian" brain and the "Paleomammalian" or limbic system of the triune brain theory made for some great pop psychology. Interesting that it pans out on the actual cellular level, though. I've always envied neurological researchers. Chopping up brains all day sounds like fun. You're confusing data minimization (the website doesn't get your name from the token) with unlinkability (nobody can connect your activity to your identity). The *token itself* doesn't reveal your identity, but it leaks data in a wide variety of ways. Even if you browse via a proxy, if the authorization service gets a token directly from your phone, it has now correlated your phone with your proxy-browsed history. A very common means to do secure authentication these days is SIM-based wireless PKI, wheren it has now connected you to your phone number. To governments, it offers a broad range of brand new vectors to correlate you to an identity if you, say, mention you had an abortion or criticize your local strongman leader, since they control the backend infrastructure that establishes the connection between the token and the identity. And not just at signup time every real-world digital credential must be able to be revoked if the phone is lost or stolen, or credentials expire or are revoked. So how does how does the open-source app prove a credential is still valid without "phoning home"? If the app checks a revocation status list, then ISPs, CDNs, or state security services can correlate the exact millisecond of queries to the revocation server with the submission of a verification token to a platform - each time it's submitted. Assuming that they can't just get it with more direct means than timing attacks. Also, you cannot just "read the code". The frontend is open source, but what is being done on the backend is not. You have no clue what logging polciies, database schemas, operational security, etc the "designated national issuers" or servers handling the credential revocation are using, and what government intrusion has been forced on them. I'd feel a more comfortable if it were all happening from Brussels but handing it over to individual states, some of which at any given point will be authoritarian, is just handing them an easy new vector to unmask people. As for your "Such systems are implemented in many different ways by member states for other purposes already." - yes, for filing taxes, healthcare, online baking, etc - things that you intend to do and must do under your legal identity. Not for anonymous conversations on the internet. Your argument basically reverts to "But people already do things non-anoymously on the internet , which reinforces my point that yes, this taking something from the "anonymous" category and putting it into the "easily unmasked" category. If you think this won't be abused, I cannot help you. The Theory Behind Government’s Cruelest Paperwork
2026-09-19

The Theory Behind Government’s Cruelest Paperwork

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-19

Visit Original Website
Gabfest Reads is a monthly series from the hosts of Slate’s Political Gabfest podcast. This month, David Plotz talks with journalist Annie Lowrey about her new book The Time Tax: How the Government Wastes Our Time—and How to Fix It. In this excerpt, Lowrey explains the “ordeal mechanism theory,” the decades-old idea behind why some government benefits are designed to be difficult to access, and why she argues it ends up failing the people who need help most. This partial transcript has been edited and condensed for clarity. David Plotz: I had never heard of this concept in your book and I was like, ‘That’s an amazing concept.’ Can you just describe what the “ordeal mechanism theory” is? There’s some context in which I guess it could be right. But no, this turns out to be wrong because very often people who are very vulnerable, very low income just can’t make it through the ordeal. They don’t have phones, like up-to-date phones; they don’t have computers; they don’t have Wi-Fi; they don’t have stable addresses. Plus, their lives are really stressful, so it makes it really hard to get through things. But anyway, so that’s the ordeal mechanism theory.” Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. America Hates A.I. Donald Trump Loves It. What Is Going On Here?
2026-09-19

America Hates A.I. Donald Trump Loves It. What Is Going On Here?

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-19

Visit Original Website
The Surge is your weekly political sanity check: What matters, what might, and what’s just political theater. Sign up here to get it in your inbox. Welcome to this week’s edition of the Surge, Slate’s politics newsletter that welcomes Mitch McConnell back to the Senate. Is Lindsey Graham next? If there was but one thing that happened this week, it was politics. New Hampshire, sulky and brooding, thinks it’s too “cool” to participate in a Democratic-wave election. Did Pete Hegseth almost get impeached this week? A Texas Republican candidate caused a proper foofaraw after getting deeply upset by a photo of brown people enjoying a football game. And, please, we are still welcoming feedback on the changes around this newsletter. Let us know. But first: How much are these killer robots paying Donald Trump to like them? (Actually, that’s probably discoverable in public filings …) Last week’s calls from inside the house that A.I. companies are at risk of losing control of their monsters and killing everyone—true or not!—have continued to sweep the news. Congress rolled up its sleeves and got to work devising plans for slow-moving task forces that may or may not ever materialize, before leaving D.C. until November. The salience of A.I. concerns has skyrocketed overnight. Polls show deep concerns among the electorate about A.I. advances. This isn’t going to abate as an issue over time, either. With each passing day, A.I. will become a bigger and bigger part of everyone’s lives. Soon, you won’t be able to spit without hitting an A.I. agent coming at you with a knife. (Or, well, showing you a helpful recipe using ingredients you have sitting around the house.) In short, we’ve never seen a bigger layup issue for populists, left and right, heading down the stretch of an election season. Which makes it all the stranger that the guy who ushered in the new populist era, Donald Trump, doesn’t have it in him to say a cross word about these machines at all. He’ll only chastise those who do. “I am the Hoax Buster, and I’m right now breaking another Hoax—That AI is going to take over, consume, and destroy the World, and that Robots will be marching into our Cities, and getting rid of us all!” he posted this week. “This is even wilder than the RUSSIA, RUSSIA, RUSSIA HOAX, or the Global Warming Scam.” In a separate screed regarding the need to regulate A.I., Trump posted that the “only control or ‘guardrails’ that AI needs is a STRONG AND SMART (High IQ!) PRESIDENT, and the U.S.A. has that, in spades!” He called the A.I. warnings a “SICK conspiracy” and warned: “Conspiracy Theorists, Treasonists, Traitors, and Leakers, BEWARE!” All of this follows a late-August post responding to the data center backlash when Trump chastised voters for their concerns. “If we kill the Golden Goose,” he wrote then, “you will only have yourselves to blame.” Why can’t Trump just ride the wave here, when he’s usually so good at co-opting shrieking public panics? A few thoughts come to mind. You could say he’s genuinely concerned about being outmaneuvered by China on A.I. and that this is informed by classified briefings he gets on the matter. But he just doodles pictures of hamburgers during those. More important to him is that the tech people are nice to him, always bringing him gifts and purchasing protection by donating to his political action committees. What’s also important to him is that A.I. buildout has been responsible for most, if not all, of the GDP growth under his administration. The “Golden Goose,” then, is the Trump economy’s paper gain. It’s that last part that’s so critical given Trump’s psychology: He views the attack on the engine of growth he presides over as an attack on him. Comparing the A.I. backlash to the “RUSSIA, RUSSIA, RUSSIA HOAX” is as close to a tell as there is. There haven’t been many bad polls for Democrats across the board since Labor Day, as pollsters shift to surveys of likely voters instead of registered voters and capture the Democratic-enthusiasm edge. (Required note of caution: Sometimes polls are wrong as hell!) But there’s one race that’s standing out, much as the likeness of an old man’s face once did on Cannon Mountain. Some recent surveys show Democrat Chris Pappas and Republican John Sununu, who served in the Senate previously, locked in a tight contest in New Hampshire’s Senate race. And this week, the Cook Political Report shifted its race rating to “toss-up.” What’s going on here? Sure, New Hampshire’s not the bluest state in history, but it should be comfortably blue enough for a Democrat to win in this environment. And Pappas, a sitting congressman, should be a perfectly acceptable head of hair to complete the task. The most obvious issue is Pappas’ opponent’s last name. The Sununus are one of New Hampshire’s royal families, and John’s brother Chris is a popular former governor. Republicans have also outspent Democrats in the race as they attempt something of a sneak attack, with plenty more coming in ad reservations this fall. Republican Gov. Kelly Ayotte, meanwhile, is cruising to reelection, and could carry Sununu along with her. We thiiiink this will sort itself out based on fundamentals, and that Republicans are mostly trying to get Democrats to spend money to defend it? But there are always anomalies in even would-be wave years, and Voters might think the Senate candidate is his brother is the stuff from which anomalies are made. It’s not a situation that Democrats can ignore anymore, at least. California Gov. Gavin Newsom told CNN’s Jake Tapper while fly-fishing (?) that he would not run for president if Kamala Harris ran. He wasn’t even backed into this corner. This was just two of the fellas out there fishing, and Newsom said it with barely any prompt. “Why would I?” Newsom said, after Tapper responded with a huh? “I wouldn’t do that to her, I wouldn’t do that to you. I wouldn’t waste everyone’s time.” What that tells us is that he must believe, or know, that Harris won’t run. Because there’s no way that Newsom is not running for president. No way! He also told Tapper that he would have to check to make sure a run would be OK with his family, but they all say that—the family is always reportedly enthused (unseen, off-camera). Another possibility is that Newsom will do a sneaky li’l thing like quick-announcing his candidacy before she has a chance to announce hers. Because he’s clearly thought about how to beat her. When Tapper said that the argument against Harris would be that she’s had her shot and lost, Newsom didn’t miss a beat. “Yeah,” Newsom said, “and, I mean, that would be the approach to the campaign, certainly. That would be the case we’d make. A pretty good one, actually. That’s objectively true.” Watch out for these sneaky Californians, folks. They’re the Greatest Political Villains in America. Trump Can’t Stop Talking About Them. I Went Into Their Shadowy Lair.
2026-09-18

They’re the Greatest Political Villains in America. Trump Can’t Stop Talking About Them. I Went Into Their Shadowy Lair.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
The facade of the building betrays no affiliation—neither hammer nor sickle. At 235 W. 23rd St. in Manhattan, the lobby is similarly spartan. All the signage is in black and white, the walls various shades of gray. A private equity firm resides on the fifth floor; an A.I. firm takes up the penthouse property on eight. The third and fourth floors have tenants as well. The directory says that on the seventh floor, there is the building’s management company, an education company, two publishers, and something else: the “Offices of the CPUSA”—that is, the headquarters of the Communist Party USA. On the occasion of America’s 250th anniversary, President Donald Trump took time away from the birthday revelry to warn of a grave and imposing adversary. “Communism is a mortal threat to American liberty. It is the greatest threat to our country, including World War I, World War II, Pearl Harbor, or even 9/11,” he said on July Fourth weekend this year, in South Dakota. That’s an awfully big threat, you might think, but the president is way ahead of you. “I think it’s the greatest threat that I’ve ever seen to our country,” he told a Georgia audience a few days before. In fact, according to the Atlantic, Trump mentioned communism or communist 15 times on that Georgia soapbox, “starting less than a minute into his hour-plus speech.” All summer long he’s been worked up, not over the antifa anarchists or the DSA socialists but over the commies in particular. He’s not alone. Secretary of State Marco Rubio is also riled by the Red hunt—he’s been convening politicians and law-enforcement officers and counterterrorism experts from across the globe to combat the “transnational threat.” And recently confirmed Secretary of Homeland Security Markwayne Mullin has seen the communists on our very own liberty-loving shores. He went on Fox News with a big-time scoop: He had identified the Communist Party logo on a “No Kings” rally flyer. “No Kings rally is literally sponsored by the Communist Party,” he ascertained. Red Scare politics are back, about that there is no question. But where, exactly, are those scary Reds who have infiltrated and organized and outflanked the administration? Certainly, it seems, they are everywhere. But individually, they are extremely difficult to find. They are not doing high-profile sabotage of corporations; they are not shooting it out in the coal mines. One place they definitely are, though, is in this building in Manhattan, right out there in the open for anyone to see. And so, on a late morning in late August, I buzzed the seventh floor and rode the elevator straight into the belly of the beast. In the middle of the CPUSA office, sitting at a table in a conference room with four walls of glass, was Joe Sims, the party’s co-chair, wearing a black newsboy cap. In front of him stood a walker. Across the table was another comrade, youthful office assistant Brooklyn Crawford. Usually, it would be slightly more bustling than this, Sims assured me. But, as it was late August, these were the only two people in the office, save for the odd delivery guy, dropping off a package. Already, this was a deeper trip into the inner sanctum than anyone in the Trump administration had managed. CPUSA co-chair Sims told me that despite the president’s pugilism, there had not yet been any Justice Department demands, nor congressional subpoenas, nor even right-wing agitators out front. “It’s not a crime to be a Communist in this country,” he said. Around the office were vestiges of times gone by. The walls were decorated with a framed cartoon of Vladimir Lenin* sweeping kings and capitalist fat cats from the Earth, and a charcoal drawing of a man with a mallet pointing to a bull’s-eye reading “$60,000,” part of a onetime fundraising drive. There was a stylized poster of Che Guevara celebrating a little-known holiday on Oct. 8—Day of the Heroic Guerrilla. Elsewhere, Soviet realist–style paintings hung. Gifts from Cuba, a plate from the Communist Party of Vietnam (given “with compliments”), and various statuettes and busts adorned the countertops and bookshelves. Framed photos of bygone leadership. There was a small kitchen, with a sign taped to the fridge: “Please clean up after yourself no maid service here!!!” Out of the back windows was a view of the Edge NYC, the flagship high-rise property of Hudson Yards, with its protuberant observation deck. Out of the front windows was the distinctive facade of the Chelsea Hotel. The office, Crawford told me, had been designed for transparency and decked out with sustainable materials: bamboo, linoleum tables made from linseed oil, recycled carpeting, and the glass-walled conference room, all in service of an environmentally aligned approach. I settled into a chair between comrade Sims and comrade Crawford and asked them about the party’s political program, the path toward revolution—a winding path, no doubt, but one that has required incredible endurance. The party has survived the Red Scare of the 1950s, the Cold War, and the Soviet collapse, and is somehow over 100 years old. And now it is readying another Red Scare. Let’s get this out of the way quickly: The USA Communist Party, contra Trump and Co., is not a major player in American politics these days, behind the scenes or out front. It works with local tenants’ groups, it boosts candidates, it piggybacks on the Democratic Socialists of America sometimes, but sometimes not. Its numbers have grown over the past decade, but not overwhelmingly. But arguably more important than the art collection or the political strategy, and perhaps more important even than the dues collection, in explaining the Communist Party’s unlikely longevity, is the building we were in itself. Because the CPUSA is not merely a tenant holding down an office on a surprisingly posh block of Manhattan’s very gilt-edged neighborhood of Chelsea. The CPUSA is the owner of the office. In fact, the Communist Party is owner of the entire building at 235 W. 23rd St., a flagship commercial property now valued at nearly $10 million. And one of the party’s essential functions at the moment, as it has been for the past 20 years, is something Trump could well understand: It is a commercial landlord, collecting rents from commercial tenants on the building’s other floors. The Communists and the president, those bitter adversaries, might have more in common than they think. But unlike Trump, the Communists really, really hate talking about it. The story of the CPUSA cannot be told without getting into the quirks and particularities of Gus Hall. In 1959 Hall was first elected secretary general of the party, a title he held until his death in 2000. Hall ran for president (of the United States) four times: twice in the 1970s, twice in the 1980s. His best finish came in 1976, when he netted just shy of 59,000 votes, though the exact count is contested. He was the genuine artifact, the son of an impoverished Minnesota miner, and he served as a lumberjack and a steelworker in his youth. Hall led the party through the exceedingly challenging times of the Cold War era, the aftermath of McCarthyism, and the subsequent collapse of the Soviet Union. In some sense, it is a miracle that the party survived, though it has held on in a deeply diminished fashion. Although Hall long maintained that the CPUSA was a fully independent entity, a Moscow newspaper reported in 1992 that Hall had been the recipient of $40 million in Soviet assistance between 1971 and 1990. “The struggle between those who own the wealth and those whose labor produces the wealth is one flaw in capitalism that will lead to socialism,” Hall said in 1996. And Hall took his own sermonizing to heart. In the late 1970s, during that period of Soviet patronage, Hall decided to get into real estate. He purchased the building on 23rd Street, according to public records, in the 1970s, taking out a $150,000 mortgage on the property at an 8 percent interest rate. That wasn’t his only shrewd money move. There were other properties Hall and the party owned too. Sims, meanwhile, told me that Hall also got the party into the stock market. Even in the 1970s, Hall had a reputation for “playing around with stocks,” Sims said. Under the leadership of Hall and national Chairman Henry Winston, the party raised capital funds from comrades, which were then invested. So while his politicking may have left something to be desired—the party bled membership while he celebrated North Korea and various Stalinist ideas—his asset accumulation proved visionary. “We have investments,” Sims told me, declining to further elaborate on the portfolio. For a long time, the CPUSA occupied the entire Chelsea building, despite a dwindling headcount. And when the 1907-built edifice required maintenance, some party members did not view it as a blessing. In a September 2001 report to the board, a year after Hall’s death, the party took stock of its holding. “Our building is a wonderful asset, a real treasure for our Party and the working class movement of which we are all immensely proud. It’s situated in a prime location, midtown Manhattan, worth million[s] in today’s market.” But having five-digit annual operating expenses “puts a tremendous burden on Party finances,” the report found. “You often hear that the building has a shabby appearance. Unfortunately we would have to admit this is all too true. The reality is we can’t keep on top of it. The building has been under capitalized for years.” Finally, the report resolves: Though the party “still [has] to be alert to the enemy,” the “decline in anti-communism has created new opportunities to utilize our building in a new way.” In other words: to lease it out. And so the revolutionaries became rent-seekers. Hall once famously said he was forced to buy his home in Yonkers—under his wife’s name, in 1960, for $21,000—because no landlord “would rent to a Communist leader.” Now the Communists were on the other side. By 2007, they were in the process of withdrawing from eight to four floors, after having spent $1 million on renovations, and renting the other four out. By 2026, they had vacated seven of eight. On the seventh floor, the new Red Square had not quite arrived. But it is worth being clear that this is not simply White House bluster or Fox News filler. Republicans in Congress, on the Ways and Means Committee, have subpoenaed various left-wing groups as part of an investigation into foreign funding linked to the Chinese Communist Party. But that dogged search for connections to the Chinese Communist Party has not led Republicans to the American Communist Party. “So far, thankfully, no,” Sims said. “As long as we pay our taxes, pay our electrical bill, I think we’re OK. “We have what we call fraternal ties with other Communist Parties, but we don’t have any economic relationships. Nor do these other groups, by the way.” When Vermont Sen. Bernie Sanders sparked the left-wing reawakening, in 2016, the CPUSA membership was nearly even with that of the fledgling Democratic Socialists of America, both sleepy holdovers of a bygone political moment. According to Gus Hall’s obituary in the New York Times in 2000, Communist Party enrollment “had dwindled to about 3,000, down from a peak of about 66,000 in 1939.” In 1962 a full 17 percent of the membership were reported to be federal informants. “Since 2016, we’ve recruited 20,000 people that have applied for membership in the party,” Sims told me, though the party has long suffered from overstatement when it comes to self-reported membership numbers. “I think about 4,000 people have joined since 2022,” he added, a more modest addendum. According to the party’s website, about a third of those pay dues. But even if that’s a generous figure, it’s paltry compared to the growth of the DSA over that same time period. In a decade, the group has gone from roughly 6,000 members to 120,000, marking some of its biggest gains in the past year or so. “I’m jealous,” Sims said. Critically, the DSA doesn’t just have members; it also has elected officials. At last count, there were six DSA members in the New York state Assembly, three DSA state senators, and four DSAers on the New York City Council. Bigger, perhaps, than all is the New York City mayor’s mansion, where democratic socialist Zohran Mamdani resides. And that’s just in New York: Soon, there will likely be DSA-affiliated members of Congress from Colorado, Pennsylvania, Michigan, and more. That is high on the to-do list for the CPUSA. “We got away from running candidates in the 1980s,” Sims told me. “That’s something we’d like to get back to.” “If we were to win an election, in a major city, or even in a midsize city … it would’ve been on the front page of the New York Times. It’s one thing for a Socialist to win, but for a Communist, oh my God,” he said. Running candidates is not the only area in which the group differs with the DSA, despite the shared affinity for the color red. On some issues there is agreement, and the two groups work in coalition. “Not an ideological configuration but a practical one,” said Sims. DSA groups sometimes come and use the office when there is strategic alignment and a need for a venue. But not always. “On some things we don’t agree, like on housing. We’re for public housing. They’re for a scheme which goes in the direction of privatization,” said Sims, referring to RAD/PACT, a policy supported by the mayor that would see Section 9, or traditional public housing, converted to Section 8, which the city owns but doesn’t directly fund or manage. Another point of difference would be the property itself. While property ownership has been a controversy-causer in the DSA of late—the New York Post has sought, perhaps effectively, to gin up outrage over DSA co-Chair Gustavo Gordillo’s ownership of a Brooklyn home purchased by his parents—the CPUSA is not losing sleep over implications of hypocrisy with regard to its own property ownership. “People outside of leftist spaces will be like, ‘How do you call yourself a Communist—you have an iPhone?’ We all live in a society where we’re trying to live and survive and participate,” said Crawford. For now, the party sees the Republican Party in a creep toward fascism, and forestalling that is a top priority. As such, the Communists remain interested in helping Democrats win elections. That means voter turnout, as well as coalition-building on certain issues. “We’re not so much interested in a Democratic victory for its own sake, but we’re for pushing back that danger as much as possible. We live in the real world, and in the real world there are differences between the two parties on issues that affect working people,” Sims said. A Democratic majority in the House would, among other things, probably cool off the Red Scare redux. “The Democratic Party, though it has a lot of weaknesses, they haven’t joined the choir,” he said. Flipping the House to a Democratic majority would get the gavel away from Republican Ways and Means Committee Chair Jason Smith and put an end to his Joe McCarthy impression. This office, though quiet on this Thursday, does get used—it is not just “a museum of history,” as Gus Hall used to call it. The Young Communist League meets there; some tenant groups from local public housing units will gather there as well. The party continues to publish People’s World, the publication once (and perhaps better) known as the Daily Worker. Is the publication funded primarily by rents raised from the office building? I asked. “You keep coming back to the building,” Sims said. “No.” It’s true—I kept coming back to the building. I couldn’t help it. A website mocked up by commercial real estate brokerage RDE Advisors celebrates its many advantages. “12’ ceilings throughout, with a spectacular 16’ ceiling penthouse on the 8th floor,” it reads. “What could 235 W. 23rd become?” asks another part of the site. “AI Tech Headquarters” and “Fashion Agency” are among the suggestions. The building boasts 55,362 square feet of rentable space, roughly 6,766 per floor. The site claims that all of the floors are vacant/available or soon will be, including the Communist Party’s seventh, which is not correct. The asking price—$39 per square foot, per year—looked like a bargain when compared cursorily to other available office space in the neighborhood listed online. “This is one of the few remaining opportunities in Manhattan to consolidate your entire organization under one roof,” the site says. “The landlord will consider leasing floors individually.” Crawford, the assistant, clarified to me that they would rent to most people, “though we wouldn’t rent to Turning Point USA.” The leasing agents didn’t respond to my requests for comment. I reached out to all of the tenants as well, to ask what it was like to have the Communist Party as a landlord. Only Ross Cameron, co-founder of Lyric Capital Group, a private equity firm focused on music royalties, housed on four, responded. “This email made me laugh out loud! Didn’t know there was a connection with the owner to the Communist Party and honestly didn’t know there was still an American Communist Party!” he said. How Windows turned months of inactivity into a 7-hour update hostage situation
2026-09-20

How Windows turned months of inactivity into a 7-hour update hostage situation

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-20

Visit Original Website
Windows is getting more efficient. The updates, not so much Microsoft has a problem not entirely of its own making: The time it takes to bring a system back up to date after a few months of slumber. The company is keen to talk up how efficient it plans to make Windows. Not a day goes by without either a Microsoft representative or a fan insisting that Windows 11 is a lot snappier now than it used to be. (Usually they skirt the unfortunate truth that the Windows of yesteryear generally flies on modern hardware compared with the lumbering of today's software). Microsoft recently claimed that it is working to shrink Windows' footprint to the point where 8 GB would be enough to run things acceptably. We can only hope that one shard of light piercing the gloom of today's memory shortages and rising prices is that developers might prioritize efficient RAM use alongside slinging more ads or adding features few users require. However, this vulture's experience this week indicates that there is another area that needs addressing, and that is the number of updates slung out by vendors, and not just Microsoft. A few days ago, I wrote a story about a new way to skip the Microsoft account requirement during setup for the Home edition of Windows 11. At The Register, we like to test these claims thoroughly, so I fired up an x64 Windows laptop to check them out on real hardware, which is where the problems began. We've written before about the curse of updates, but this machine was running Windows 11 and had been up to date less than a year ago. How long could it take to get to the point where it would boot without Windows Update bleating about missed patches? The answer: about seven hours. Along with the huge array of updates for Windows components and the inevitable insistence that a new version of Microsoft's flagship operating system was needed, there were multiple firmware and driver updates and plenty of restarts. And, of course, there was the progress bar of lies and the percentage counter of deceit. In the decades since Microsoft first inflicted progress bars on Windows users, it has singularly failed to make them accurately reflect the, er, progress. "Yeah, so I'm going to stick at 65 percent for about an hour and then jump to 98 percent in 30 seconds before hanging there for another hour. Lol." That this behavior has endured for decades is beyond disappointing. Redmond is far from the only offender, but Windows' ubiquity makes it one of the most visible. To be clear, this was an ordinary Windows device, running a production version of the operating system. It just hadn't been turned on for a few months. The experience highlights a challenge Microsoft and its vendors must address. It's great that, at long last, people are considering code efficiency rather than simply flinging more hardware at badly written applications to make them run acceptably. But attention must also be paid to the constant flow of updates that seem to stem from an "ah well, we can always fix it in production" attitude that pervades the tech industry. Several years ago, the chief of a DevOps project was baffled when I asked him about testing and validation before release. Surely unit testing was sufficient? And the joy of modern development practices meant that if problems arose, an update could be swiftly rolled out. All of which assumes customers will tolerate continual updates, and a potential hours-long wait if, for some reason, they give it a few months between updates. The tech industry is waking up to the need for efficiencies, or at the very least Microsoft is. It also needs to deal with the scourge of update tsunamis. It's all well and good to brag about making your software run on limited resources until an interminable wait undermines the boast while the various widgets and components of a device insist on spending hours giving themselves a thorough update. Shaving a few seconds off the startup time means nothing if there are pages of updates awaiting the unwary. ® High time to stop kicking the security can down the road, investor tells The Reg Report says estimates focused on GPUs miss the mountains of power, cooling, and networking gear destined for scrap PARTNER CONTENT: Pairs block and adjacent file workloads with independent scaling of performance and capacity From Waterfox to LibreWolf and Pale Moon, there's a browser for almost every Mozilla dissenter Downloading a model is increasingly easy. Agentic security is the billion-dollar challenge for some clever startup to solve
2026-09-19

Agentic security is the billion-dollar challenge for some clever startup to solve

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-19

Visit Original Website
High time to stop kicking the security can down the road, investor tells The Reg When it comes to AI models, security functions as an afterthought, as evidenced by increased instances of agents hacking organizations and people, and other security mishaps with agents gone rogue. There's also an opportunity here for companies to offer new solutions. This should not come as a shock to anyone, according to cybersecurity investors and accelerator executives. “On one hand, we shouldn’t be surprised that increasingly capable agents are finding creative and sometimes unexpected ways to accomplish their objectives,” Matt Hartman, chief strategy officer at Merlin Group, told The Register. “On the other, we can’t accept harmful behavior as inevitable or unmanageable.” It’s the same story that plays out with every emerging technology, from laptops to cloud, said Todd Graham, managing partner at Microsoft’s M12 venture fund. “Every time we've built a new piece of infrastructure, we've conveniently forgotten the security,” Graham told The Register. Herein lies the opportunity for early-stage security companies. “If laptops were default secure, we wouldn't have CrowdStrike,” Graham said. “If the cloud was default secure, we wouldn't have Wiz. If identity wasn't default secure, we wouldn't have a bunch of Active Directory add-ons and Okta.” When it comes to AI security, “a lot of ships are going to rise with this tide,” he added. What’s different with AI is the speed at which models are advancing. While companies adopted cloud technologies over a period of years, organizations are moving full speed ahead to incorporate agents and other AI tools into their production environments and allow them to access the most business-critical data and applications. Yet they don’t have a strong handle on how to manage, secure, or even identify the agents that are already roving about their systems. “This is a transition happening month over month, and given the rate of change we’re seeing in the market, I’m in no way or shape surprised that this issue has come to a head in a rather dramatic fashion,” Graham said. “The incidents that have occurred - I’m not going to defend them, but they should be a wake up call. This is a moment in time where we need to insert security, and we're just going to have to insert it faster.” Hartman joined Merlin after a lengthy career in federal cybersecurity, including senior roles at the US Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency. In his private-sector role, he helps determine which early- to growth-stage cybersecurity companies the group invests in, and then works with these firms to scale their technology across government, critical infrastructure, and other highly regulated markets. “We’re particularly interested in the security layer that governs agent behavior: identity for non-human actors, clear limits on what they can access and do, and an audit trail for actions taken on an agency’s behalf,” he said. “Agencies aren’t just asking how to adopt agents, they’re asking how to constrain them and prove what one did at 2 AM on a Tuesday.” But while “the opportunity is enormous,” startup founders need to do more than just build an agentic AI security product. “AI has made it faster and cheaper than ever to build a product, so the bar for differentiation keeps rising,” Hartman said. “As the cost of building technology falls, the value shifts toward differentiated capabilities and the ability to take them to market. Founders who can do both have a real opportunity to define this category.” Graham also says that end-users are looking for agentic identity and governance products - “I truly believe someone is going to build the next Okta, just as SaaS generated Okta,” - but adds he sees several founders “thinking way too small.” “I’m seeing a lot of companies that are solving a sliver of the problem,” Graham said. “And the reality is, if I'm a CISO for a Fortune 500 company, no way I'm going to go buy 15 things to do one thing. If you look at the standard identity stack that we’ve had for humans for quite some time, it has governance, you know, access control, authorization, access. For agents, someone's going to have to come to us with a solution that does all of the things.” When it comes to non-human identities, however, that’s a really big, tough ask. Service accounts remain a prime target for hackers because they typically have high privileges and passwords that never expire. Securing these non-human accounts still plagues security teams - and that’s even before agents entered the mix. Beyond agentic identity, AI endpoint security - think of this as CrowdStrike for AI - is another area ripe for inventive startups, Graham said. He says it’s a challenge he would personally tackle as a founder, but he’s been banned from starting any more companies, so that’s not going to happen. “If you have a breach, and you know you get hauled in front of Congress to explain why you didn't have antivirus turned on [or] EDR, you’re going to add AI endpoint pretty quickly to that list,” Graham said. “It feels like a very hot area that is still early enough, if someone wanted to build a quality solution.” Existing endpoint and antivirus vendors “will absolutely” build products to fill this void, he added. “But it does feel like again a moment in time where disruption is coming for everyone, especially those that have you know pre-existing commitments to go solve.” Graham admits he is “worried” about the recent real-world bad behavior by AI agents. “If left unencumbered, if left to its own devices, I am very concerned about where the endpoint is for this,” he said. Still, he’s “comforted” because he’s seen this scenario play out before, with security scrambling to keep up with infrastructure development. Graham also was "pleasantly surprised” by Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei’s now infamous “We Must Pace the Frontier” essay. “I'm not taking the cynical view that it's some sort of grand conspiracy to get around antitrust,” he told us. “I'm a believer that AI is this awesome tool that is going to fundamentally change a lot of lives for the better. But we’ve got to put in the work now,” Graham said. “We've kicked the security can down the road long enough, and now we need to solve it.” ® High time to stop kicking the security can down the road, investor tells The Reg Report says estimates focused on GPUs miss the mountains of power, cooling, and networking gear destined for scrap PARTNER CONTENT: Pairs block and adjacent file workloads with independent scaling of performance and capacity From Waterfox to LibreWolf and Pale Moon, there's a browser for almost every Mozilla dissenter Downloading a model is increasingly easy. AI boom could leave an e-waste trail that wraps 6 times around Earth
2026-09-19

AI boom could leave an e-waste trail that wraps 6 times around Earth

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-19

Visit Original Website
Report says estimates focused on GPUs miss the mountains of power, cooling, and networking gear destined for scrap AI infrastructure could generate enough electronic waste by 2050 to fill a line of shipping containers stretching around Earth six times, according to a report that argues existing estimates drastically understate the problem. Previous estimates have focused chiefly on servers and accelerators such as GPUs, which account for just 13 percent of a datacenter's equipment by weight, the report says. Once power, networking, cooling, and other infrastructure are included, the total could be 40 to 60 times higher than the most widely cited academic projections. The report identifies five equipment categories comprising networking, power distribution, backup power systems, servers plus accelerators, and cooling. Together, these add up to about 7,000 metric tons for a reference 100 MW AI bit barn, much of which will have to be replaced when the facility is upgraded, it argues. The report, How Big Is the AI Waste Wave? [PDF], comes from the Basel Action Network (BAN), a nonprofit organization named after the Basel Convention, which controls international movements of hazardous waste and seeks to prevent its transfer from developed to developing countries. BAN's model starts with what the industry says it intends to build and calculates the potential waste implied by that expansion. It estimates that AI infrastructure will generate between 395 million and 617 million tonnes of e-waste from 2025 to 2050. The report says that would fill between 15 million and 23 million shipping containers. Placing 20 million of them end to end would create a line about 244,000 km long, or roughly six times Earth's circumference. The estimate rests on several assumptions. The model supposes that the copper, steel, cooling and power distribution equipment, networking gear, and computing hardware in the latest AI datacenters will be decommissioned within years rather than decades as the technology becomes obsolete. For example, the report says that when a facility replaces racks drawing between 5 and 15 kW with the 50-140 kW racks required for AI workloads, much of its power infrastructure must be ripped out and replaced. A single 100 MW facility contains about 2,700 tonnes of copper in its cabling and busbars alone. GPUs and other AI accelerators are replaced every two to three years, BAN claims, compared with five to seven years for traditional general-purpose servers. The model assumes that owners replace entire servers when upgrading to the latest generation of Nvidia GPUs, for example. BAN argues this is plausible because the OEMs supplying hyperscalers tend to ship complete, preconfigured systems. Other equipment has different replacement cycles: three to four years for networking infrastructure, eight years for power distribution, and five years for both backup power and cooling systems. The 46-page paper is the first in a series. The second will examine how reuse, refurbishment, and repurposing could reduce the volume of e-waste. The third will consider its potential toxicity, including contamination by perfluoroalkyl and polyfluoroalkyl substances (PFAS), while the fourth will examine ways to mitigate the risks. No hyperscaler, government, or international body has published a plan for handling AI-driven e-waste on the scale projected, the report says. It adds that there is insufficient infrastructure to process even current volumes safely. "To date the environmental debate around AI has focused on electricity, carbon and water while largely overlooking what happens to the hardware itself," said BAN founder and chief of strategic direction Jim Puckett. "If companies and governments do not begin planning for this new waste tsunami, today's AI buildout could become an even more cataclysmic toxic waste crisis than we are already experiencing." ® High time to stop kicking the security can down the road, investor tells The Reg Report says estimates focused on GPUs miss the mountains of power, cooling, and networking gear destined for scrap PARTNER CONTENT: Pairs block and adjacent file workloads with independent scaling of performance and capacity From Waterfox to LibreWolf and Pale Moon, there's a browser for almost every Mozilla dissenter Downloading a model is increasingly easy.
